NextGen Optical Transfer Design

"Design and develop a next generation and production grade QR code-like system that allows for transferring megabytes up to potentially even gigabytes. We need an app for Android based on Kotlin that allows you to be able to properly take any form of data including APK files, etc. I must be able to use the app to be able to take any data and create a scannable image like barcodes and QR codes, but I want to create a leap forward in technology and I want to use new methods and techniques including a mixture of shapes, symbols, numbers, letters, etc. Do extremely deep and analytical research analysis and provide comprehensive in-depth and detailed suggestions for the optimal way to do this. I am open to anything."

Below is a deep technical research analysis and production‑oriented design recommendation for a next‑generation optical data transport system on Android. The central conclusion is that **a single scannable image cannot practically carry megabytes or gigabytes**; the only viable path to MB–GB transfer is a **multi‑frame, fountain‑coded optical stream** paired with a **much denser symbol design** than QR. The “leap forward” you want is best achieved by combining high‑density spatial/color modulation, modern channel coding, native decoding, and machine‑learning‑assisted detection.

---

## 1. Feasibility verdict and theoretical ceiling

### 1.1 Why a single image cannot reach megabytes

Even the largest QR code (Version 40) stores only **2,953 bytes**, and its areal density is roughly **0.58 bits/mm²** at 300 dpi. Experimental high‑capacity codes push single‑frame capacity further, but still into the kilobytes, not megabytes:

- **LIBCIMBAR** achieves **7,500 bytes per 1024×1024 image** after error correction, using 16 shapes and 4–8 colors per tile.
- **SSMC** claims **over 3× the areal data density of QR** by encoding in color and geometry on an aperiodic Penrose tiling.
- **NestedDot** theoretically exceeds QR V40 capacity by a factor of 25 at depth‑3, but its authors explicitly note that error correction, physical readability, and standardisation remain open.

These are impressive for printed labels or short payloads, but a 1 MB file would still require **hundreds to thousands of frames**. Therefore, **the product must be a temporal stream, not a single image**.

### 1.2 How fast can a screen‑to‑camera link actually go?

The optical channel is not the bottleneck in theory. Capacity studies estimate that **tens of Mbps** are possible using a smartphone camera even when the code occupies only 15 % of the camera frame. Practical prototypes are already in the **~100–130 KB/s** range:

- **Decimen** reports **128 KB/s** phone‑to‑phone and supports files up to 64 MB with fountain‑coded QR streams.
- **LIBCIMBAR** sustains **~106 KB/s** (852 kbit/s) with 4‑color 8×8 tiles at 30/155 ECC on a Snapdragon 625.
- **ERSCC** demonstrates a **16× throughput increase** over conventional rolling‑shutter solutions and **1.8×** over color‑barcode systems.

With a denser symbol, higher frame rate, parallel code placement, and native decoding, **1–5 MB/s** is a realistic medium‑term target. Gigabyte transfers then become a matter of time and reliability rather than a fundamental impossibility.

---

## 2. Core architecture: the only production‑viable stack

A screen‑to‑camera link has **no back‑channel**. You cannot ask the sender to retransmit a lost frame. The architecture must therefore absorb loss at the coding layer and reconstruct the file from **any sufficient set of received frames**.

The recommended stack has six layers:

| Layer | Function | Key technology |
|---|---|---|
| **1. Pre‑processing** | Compression, chunking, integrity | zstd / XZ; SHA‑256; optional AES‑GCM |
| **2. Fountain coding** | Loss‑tolerant packetisation | **RaptorQ (RFC 6330)** or **Luby transform (LT)** codes |
| **3. Symbol modulation** | Convert coded packets into visual frames | Custom high‑density symbol (Section 4) |
| **4. Display streaming** | Present frames at high rate | 30–120 FPS; parallel code tiles per frame |
| **5. Capture & decode** | Camera pipeline and frame recovery | CameraX + native decoder pool + ML detection |
| **6. Reconstruction** | Fountain decode, decompress, verify | RaptorQ/LT peeling; SHA‑256 check |

**Fountain coding is non‑negotiable.** It removes the need for acknowledgements and turns dropped frames into a time cost rather than a correctness failure. **AirFerry** uses RaptorQ with a 60 FPS QR stream, ~32 MiB segments, and a **4‑code parallel mode** that roughly quadruples throughput. **Decimen** uses Luby transform codes and notes that the receiver collects **any ~K·1.15 distinct frames in any order** and peels the file out. This is the only sane foundation for a production system.

---

## 3. Next‑generation symbol design: the “mixture of shapes, symbols, numbers, letters”

The symbol layer is where you can genuinely leap beyond QR. The optimal approach is **multi‑dimensional modulation**: use geometry, colour, and symbol alphabet jointly, rather than treating the symbol as a binary pixel grid.

### 3.1 Geometric alphabet: more than two states per cell

QR uses two states per module (black/white). High‑capacity codes use **multi‑shape tiles**:

- **LIBCIMBAR** uses **16 possible symbols per tile** (4 bits) plus 4–8 colours (2–3 bits), giving **6–7 bits per tile**.
- **4SQR** uses **four distinct geometric shapes** to increase density, and a lightweight neural decoder achieves 100 % accuracy on synthetic and real datasets with **~6 MB memory** and **~65 ms decoding time**.
- **Hexatess** uses a **hexagonal grid**, gaining **~15.5 % packing density over a square grid** at equal module size, and supports a **continuously selectable Reed–Solomon budget from 5 % to 90 %**.

A production symbol should therefore use a **small alphabet of visually distinct geometric primitives** (e.g., 8 or 16 shapes) rather than binary squares. This directly increases bits per cell without shrinking the module size, which is critical for camera readability.

### 3.2 Chromatic multiplexing: colour as a parallel channel

Colour is the easiest dimension to exploit on an emissive screen, because you do not face the print‑scan colour‑gamut problems that plague printed colour barcodes. **JAB Code** uses **eight colours** and achieves approximately **three times the data density of QR/DataMatrix/Aztec**, with a redundant colour palette embedded in the symbol for robustness. **HD2DC** uses 5 or 8 channel colours with 10–30 % Reed–Solomon error correction and remains robust under lossy compression.

For a screen‑to‑camera system, **8 colours is a practical starting point**; 16 colours are possible but require per‑frame colour calibration. Every frame should include a **reference colour palette** and the decoder should work in a perceptually uniform space (e.g., CIELAB) with adaptive clustering, not raw RGB thresholds.

### 3.3 Aperiodic tiling: the strongest “leap forward” concept

**Spectral‑Structured Matrix Code (SSMC)** abandons the grid entirely and encodes data in the **colour spectrum of microdots arranged on an aperiodic Penrose tiling**. It achieves **>3× QR areal density**, requires **no finder patterns**, is **rotation‑invariant by construction**, and maintains **>95 % decode success under 40 % occlusion**, a regime where QR fails completely.

This is arguably the most compelling next‑generation direction for a production system because it solves two problems at once: density and robustness. However, it is a research‑grade format; a production implementation would need a complete encoder/decoder specification and extensive validation.

### 3.4 Recursive/nested structures: promising but not yet production‑ready

**NestedDot** uses recursive dots with 8‑valued logic and 24‑bit RGB orthogonal axes, and formal proofs show a depth‑3 100×100 nested configuration exceeding QR V40 capacity by a factor of 25. But the same authors state that **error correction, physical readability, and standardisation remain open**. Treat nested/recursive layouts as a long‑term research track, not the first production symbol.

### 3.5 Alphanumeric and symbolic alphabets

The user’s request for “numbers, letters, symbols” is best addressed at the **coding‑alphabet level**, not by literally drawing letters into cells. Use a **base‑N alphabet** where N is the number of distinguishable visual states per cell. For example, 8 shapes × 8 colours = 64 states = **6 bits per cell**. If you also modulate size or orientation, the alphabet grows further, but visual distinguishability and error resilience degrade quickly. The safest production choice is a **moderate alphabet (16–64 states)** with strong error‑correcting codes over that alphabet, rather than an aggressive alphabet that collapses under blur and perspective.

### 3.6 Recommended symbol for the first production version

A pragmatic high‑density symbol would combine:

- **Hexagonal tiling** for ~15 % density gain over square grids.
- **8–16 geometric shapes per tile** for 3–4 bits.
- **8 colours** for 3 bits.
- **Per‑frame colour palette and orientation key** (e.g., a hexagonal bullseye similar to Hexatess) for calibration and rotation invariance.
- **Adaptive ECC** (Section 4).

This yields **6–7 bits per tile**, matching or exceeding LIBCIMBAR‑class density while retaining a structured, decodable layout. For a more radical product, SSMC‑style aperiodic tiling can be the second‑generation symbol.

---

## 4. Error correction and channel coding: move beyond Reed–Solomon

Most legacy barcodes use **Reed–Solomon (RS)**. RS is ubiquitous and well understood, but it is **byte‑oriented**, while optical errors in high‑density symbols tend to occur as **1–3 bit flips or colour confusions**. LIBCIMBAR’s own documentation notes that “Reed–Solomon is not perfect for this use case” for exactly this reason.

For a next‑generation system:

- **Inner code (within a frame)**: use **LDPC** or **non‑binary LDPC over GF(q)** where q matches the symbol alphabet size. LDPC codes have been studied specifically for high‑density 2D barcode symbologies and are effective for certain parameter regimes. Non‑binary LDPC naturally handles colour/shape symbol errors as symbol errors rather than bit errors.
- **Outer code (across frames)**: use **RaptorQ** or **LT fountain codes**. RaptorQ is standardised (RFC 6330), has near‑optimal overhead, and is already used in production optical transfer systems (AirFerry).
- **Header protection**: double‑protect the frame header, as Hexatess does, so that a corrupted header does not lose the entire frame.
- **Adaptive ECC**: adjust the inner code rate dynamically based on estimated channel quality (decode success rate, blur metric, SNR). Hexatess’s continuous 5–90 % EC budget is a good model.

This two‑level scheme gives you both **within‑frame robustness** and **across‑frame loss tolerance**, which is essential for a no‑feedback optical link.

---

## 5. Android implementation in Kotlin and native code

A production‑grade decoder cannot be written purely in Kotlin at the throughput you need. The architecture should be **Kotlin for orchestration and UI, native code for the codec core**.

### 5.1 Capture pipeline

- Use **CameraX `ImageAnalysis`** with `STRATEGY_KEEP_ONLY_LATEST` to avoid frame queuing and memory blow‑up. Feeding every frame into a decoder will quickly exhaust memory or cause severe lag; keeping only the latest frame is the standard production pattern.
- Target **60 FPS** at capture; use `Camera2Interop` for manual exposure, focus, and ISO when you need deterministic behaviour.
- **Exploit rolling shutter** where possible. ERSCC shows that rolling‑shutter‑aware coding can deliver a **16× throughput improvement** over naive rolling‑shutter solutions.
- Feed frames to a **native decoder pool** running in parallel. AirFerry uses multiple ZXing‑C++ decoders in parallel to saturate high‑frame‑rate capture.

### 5.2 Decoding pipeline

- Implement the core decoder in **Rust** and expose it via **JNI**. Rust provides C‑like performance with memory safety; Android integration via `jni‑rs` is mature, and Rust native modules have been reported to reduce crash rates in multithreaded scenarios.
- Use **`rxing`** (a Rust port of ZXing) as a baseline for standard QR/barcode decoding, or implement a custom decoder for your high‑density symbol.
- Add a **lightweight deep‑learning detector** before classical decoding. **YOLO‑Barcode** achieves real‑time detection on mobile devices, is **4× faster** than the previous universal detector, and reaches **98.6 % F1** on the ZVZ‑real dataset. **4SQR‑CodeScanner** demonstrates a zero‑overhead ANN decoder with **~6 MB memory** and **~65 ms** decode time. A detector such as YOLO‑Barcode can locate and classify the symbol under difficult conditions, while the classical decoder extracts the payload.

### 5.3 Encoding and streaming

- Generate frames natively (Rust or C++) to avoid Kotlin GC pauses. The encoder must sustain **30–120 FPS** depending on symbol complexity.
- Use **parallel code placement**: place multiple independent code tiles in a single displayed frame, as AirFerry’s 4‑code mode does, to multiply throughput without increasing per‑frame decode complexity.
- Apply **compression before fountain coding**. AirFerry selects the smallest of **Raw / zstd level 1 / XZ level 9** automatically. For APK files and other already‑compressed binaries, compression may not help, but for text, logs, and many documents it dramatically reduces the number of frames.

### 5.4 File and APK handling

- Treat all input as **binary streams**. APK files are ZIP archives and are already compressed, so the system must not assume compressibility.
- Chunk large files into **~32 MiB segments** (AirFerry uses this size) and fountain‑encode each segment independently so that a transfer can resume or recover segment by segment.
- Compute **SHA‑256** of the original file and verify it after reconstruction. Do not present the file to the user until the hash matches.
- Preserve filename and MIME type. For APK files, the MIME type and package metadata can be extracted before transfer and included in the file header.

### 5.5 Kotlin libraries and tooling

- For baseline QR generation, Kotlin Multiplatform libraries such as **`qrose`** support QR, Data Matrix, Aztec, PDF417, and more. **`qrcode-kotlin`** supports versions 1–40 and all EC levels.
- However, for your custom high‑density symbol you will need a custom encoder. Use these libraries only for fallback/legacy modes.

---

## 6. Practical challenges and mitigations

| Challenge | Impact | Mitigation |
|---|---|---|
| **Perspective distortion** | Capacity drops with distance and angle; module size on sensor shrinks | Adaptive frame size; ML‑based corner detection; multi‑scale decoding; capacity‑aware rate control |
| **Rolling shutter** | Mixed frames, temporal skew | Rolling‑shutter‑aware coding (ERSCC); frame markers; discard “in‑between” frames as LIBCIMBAR does with `shakycam` |
| **Colour calibration** | Colour confusion, wrong symbol decoding | Per‑frame reference palette; white‑balance lock; work in CIELAB; adaptive clustering |
| **Motion blur** | Symbol edges smear | High frame rate, short exposure, OIS; deblurring model (Dynamsoft v11 uses a deep‑learning deblurring model) |
| **Ambient light** | Low contrast | Screen brightness boost; white background (LIBCIMBAR notes better lighting improves results) |
| **Thermal throttling** | Decode throughput drops | Native code efficiency; frame dropping; adaptive FPS; offload detection to NNAPI/GPU |
| **No back‑channel** | Cannot request retransmission | Fountain codes (RaptorQ/LT) make every frame useful; receiver collects any sufficient set |
| **Header corruption** | Entire frame lost | Double‑protected header (Hexatess); fountain layer recovers |

---

## 7. Production roadmap and concrete recommendations

### Phase 1 — Production baseline (3–6 months)

Build a **multi‑frame QR/RaptorQ optical transport** with:

- zstd/XZ compression.
- RaptorQ fountain coding (RFC 6330).
- CameraX capture at 60 FPS, native ZXing‑C++ decoder pool.
- SHA‑256 integrity verification.
- Kotlin Android app with file picker (including APK), progress UI, and resume support.

This will deliver **~100 KB/s** and is already production‑grade for many use cases. It is the safest starting point because the components are proven (AirFerry, Decimen, LIBCIMBAR all demonstrate the approach).

### Phase 2 — Differentiation (6–12 months)

Replace the QR frame with a **custom high‑density symbol**:

- Hexagonal tiling.
- 8–16 geometric shapes + 8 colours per tile.
- Per‑frame colour palette and orientation key.
- LDPC inner code + RaptorQ outer code.
- ML‑based symbol detection (YOLO‑Barcode or a lightweight custom detector).

Target **2–5× the throughput of Phase 1** (i.e., 200–500 KB/s) with equal or better robustness.

### Phase 3 — Leap (12–24 months)

Pursue **aperiodic tiling (SSMC‑style)** and **recursive/nested structures** as research tracks. SSMC’s occlusion resistance and gridless design are genuinely next‑generation, but require a full specification and extensive field testing. NestedDot’s theoretical density is attractive but its practical decoding remains open. Do not make Phase 3 the foundation of the first product; make it the differentiator for a second‑generation release.

### Key engineering recommendations

1. **Do not attempt a single‑image MB solution.** It is not physically realistic with current camera resolutions and symbol densities.
2. **Make fountain coding the first engineering priority.** Without it, dropped frames will destroy the user experience.
3. **Use Rust for the codec core.** JNI integration is mature, performance is C‑like, and memory safety reduces crash risk in the multithreaded decoder pool.
4. **Use a native decoder pool, not a single decoder.** Parallel decoding is how you saturate a 60 FPS camera feed.
5. **Include a per‑frame colour palette and orientation key.** Colour‑based symbols fail without calibration; a reference palette costs few bits and saves entire frames.
6. **Verify integrity before delivery.** A corrupted APK is worse than a failed transfer. SHA‑256 verification is mandatory.
7. **Design for fallback.** If the custom symbol fails to decode, fall back to QR frames so the user is never stuck.

---

## 8. Conclusion

The optimal path to megabyte‑ and gigabyte‑scale optical transfer on Android is **not a bigger single QR code**. It is a **multi‑frame, fountain‑coded optical stream** built on a **multi‑dimensional symbol** that uses geometry, colour, and a carefully chosen symbol alphabet. The immediate production‑grade solution is a RaptorQ‑coded QR stream with native decoding. The genuine leap forward comes from replacing QR frames with a **hexagonal, multi‑shape, multi‑colour symbol protected by LDPC and recovered by fountain coding**, and eventually exploring **aperiodic tiling** for occlusion‑resistant, gridless optical codes.

With a native Rust codec core, a Kotlin Android app using CameraX, parallel decoding, compression, and ML‑assisted detection, a system achieving **hundreds of kilobytes per second to low megabytes per second** is realistic in the near term, making gigabyte transfers a practical matter of time and reliability rather than a theoretical impossibility.

"I agree and want Aperiodic tiling, Recursive/nested structures, Chromatic multiplexing, Geometric alphabet, Fountain coding, Alphanumeric and symbolic alphabets, Hybrid Error correction and channel coding, and I also want you to actually conceptualize new method/s and/or technique/s based on gaps in what exists and what you think needs to be the new thing/s that fills those gaps. Do deep and intense research and provide comprehensive in depth and detailed results for achieving the optimal next generation method/s/technique/s"

The architecture you have selected—aperiodic geometry, recursion, chromatic and geometric modulation, fountain coding, symbolic alphabets, and hybrid FEC—is already beyond any single published optical code. The gaps are not in the individual ingredients but in **how they are made to reinforce one another**. SSMC proves aperiodic tiling works but relies on *emergent* robustness rather than explicit coding. NestedDot proves recursive capacity but explicitly lacks error-correction maturity. CCRCode proves a two-tier LDPC/rateless stack works for screen-camera links but does not co-design the symbol with the code. Below, for each domain, I identify the precise gap and propose a new technique or method that fills it, followed by a unified system architecture that binds them.

---

## 1. Aperiodic tiling: from Penrose templates to **chiral monotile coding**

### The gap

SSMC establishes the core insight: decoupling information from a Cartesian grid gives **3.14× QR areal density** and **>95% decode accuracy under 40% occlusion** without alignment markers. But SSMC uses **Penrose tiling**, which is a two-tile system, is patent-constrained, and relies on a precomputed template for decoding. More importantly, SSMC’s occlusion tolerance is **emergent**—a structural property—rather than something the coding layer can tune or extend.

The recently discovered **Hat** and **Spectre** aperiodic monotiles change the design space. The Spectre is the first **strictly chiral** monotile: no reflected copy appears in any valid tiling. Its tilings form **hierarchical fractal macro-structures** (“star triples,” “megastars”) across generations. Crucially, recent work shows that aperiodic monotiles define **quantum error-correcting codes** with exact Perron–Frobenius data for the Hat and Spectre orientation classes. The Hat code stores “one robust classical bit—the handedness of its long-range order”. This is a direct, untapped bridge between aperiodic geometry and coding theory.

### Proposed technique: **Chiral Monotile Coding (CMC)**

**Core idea:** Use the **Spectre monotile** as the primitive symbol, and exploit three independent dimensions that the tiling exposes:

1. **Tile orientation.** The Spectre appears in a finite set of orientations with known frequencies \((5\pm\sqrt{15})/10\). These orientations can carry discrete symbols.
2. **Chirality/handedness.** Because the Spectre is strictly chiral, the global handedness of a tiling patch is a robust bit that survives substantial corruption—the Hat code demonstrates exactly this robustness.
3. **Hierarchical cluster level.** Spectre tilings form **fractal macro-structures** at successive generations (star triples → megastars → higher families). Each cluster level can carry a **level identifier and parity**, enabling recursive decoding: a decoder that cannot resolve individual tiles can still read cluster-level symbols.

**Encoding.** Map the input bitstream to a tuple of (tile orientation, local chirality, cluster-level label) per tile. The cluster hierarchy provides a **natural multi-resolution code**: coarse cluster labels are protected by the emergent robustness of the aperiodic structure, while fine tile-level symbols carry the bulk payload. This directly fills the gap between SSMC’s implicit robustness and a tunable coding layer.

**Decoding.** Use the **template-based approach** from SSMC—precompute a bank of Spectre tiling seeds and match via vectorized midpoint bisection—but extend it to **hierarchical template matching**: match cluster-level templates first, then refine to tile level. Because Spectre clusters are fractal, the template bank grows sub-linearly with resolution, keeping decoding latency low.

**Why this is a leap:** It moves aperiodic optical coding from “a tiling that happens to be robust” to **a tiling whose hierarchical and chiral structure is itself the code**. The connection to aperiodic quantum codes provides a theoretical foundation that SSMC does not have.

---

## 2. Recursive/nested structures: from static nesting to **recursive fountain lattices**

### The gap

NestedDot demonstrates that a depth-*d* recursively structured dot with fanout *k* carries ≈\(k^{d-1} \times 43\) bits, and formally proves that a depth-3 100×100 configuration beats QR V40 Level L by **18×** (the abstract states “≈18×”; a separate Zenodo result claims 25× for a related depth-3 configuration). But its own authors state that **readability, standardization, and error-correction maturity still favor QR**. The nesting is purely **spatial and static**: every leaf is treated equally, and there is no mechanism for loss tolerance across recursion levels.

### Proposed technique: **Recursive Fountain Lattice (RFL)**

**Core idea:** Make recursion **temporal and probabilistic**. Instead of nesting static dots, build a **lattice of fountain-coded layers** where each recursion level is an independent LT/RaptorQ encoding of a progressively refined data representation.

**Structure.** A depth-*d* RFL symbol has *d* concentric or hierarchical regions:

- **Level 0 (root):** A coarse, heavily protected representation of the file header and a **seed** for the fountain code.
- **Level 1:** A fountain-coded layer carrying a medium-resolution payload.
- **Level *i*:** A fountain-coded layer carrying increasingly fine-grained payload.
- **Level *d*:** The finest layer, carrying the remaining repair symbols.

**Key innovation: cross-level fountain dependency.** The fountain degree distribution at level *i* is conditioned on the **decoding state** of levels 0…*i*−1. If the receiver decodes the root and level 1, it can **bootstrap** level 2 decoding with a lower overhead because the root provides side information about the source block structure. This is a form of **successive cancellation fountain decoding**, analogous to successive cancellation in polar codes but applied across spatial recursion levels.

**Unequal error protection for free.** Critical metadata (file size, hash, MIME type, APK package name) sits at the root with maximum protection. Bulk payload occupies deeper levels. If the channel degrades, the receiver still recovers the root and can report partial progress; if the channel is good, all levels decode and the file completes. This directly addresses CCRCode’s finding that **unequal error protection is essential for heterogeneous data**, but does so **structurally** rather than by adding a separate UEP code.

**Capacity scaling.** With *d* levels and fanout *k*, the total symbol count scales as \(k^d\), but the **effective fountain block length** at each level is independent. This avoids the decoding complexity explosion of a single massive fountain block while retaining the capacity advantage of recursion.

---

## 3. Chromatic multiplexing: from fixed palettes to **perceptual chromatic lattices**

### The gap

Existing color barcodes use **4–8 fixed colors** (HCCB, JAB Code, HC8C). 16 colors are theoretically possible but “require per-frame color calibration”. The fundamental problem is that screen-camera color is **not a fixed channel**: display gamut, camera white balance, ambient light, and JPEG chroma subsampling all distort the received color point. Current systems either restrict to a safe 8-color palette or use ad hoc thresholds. No production system defines the color alphabet in a **perceptually uniform space with per-frame adaptive calibration**.

### Proposed technique: **Perceptual Chromatic Lattice (PCL)**

**Core idea:** Define the color alphabet as a **lattice in a perceptually uniform color appearance space**, and transmit a **calibration constellation** in every frame that allows the receiver to estimate the current channel’s color transformation in real time.

**Color space.** Use **CIECAM02** or **CIELAB** rather than RGB. In CIELAB, Euclidean distance \(\Delta E_{00}^*\) approximates perceptual difference. SSMC already uses a threshold of \(\Delta E_{00}^* > 5.0\) as a robustness criterion for color separation. Build the alphabet as a **maximally separated packing of points** in the \((L^*, a^*, b^*)\) volume that the display can render and the camera can distinguish. For a screen-camera link, the achievable gamut is smaller than the display gamut because of camera spectral sensitivity; the packing must be optimized for the **intersection** of the two gamuts.

**Per-frame calibration constellation.** Embed a small set of **pilot patches** in fixed positions (e.g., the corners of an aperiodic seed region). These patches span the intended color alphabet. At the receiver, measure the received color of each pilot, fit a **local affine or polynomial transform** from intended CIELAB to received CIELAB, and invert it before symbol classification. This is more powerful than a single white-balance reference because it captures **non-linear and spatially varying color shifts**.

**Adaptive palette size.** Use a **rate–distortion criterion** to choose the number of active colors per frame. If the calibration constellation shows high color noise (large residual after transform fitting), fall back from 16 colors to 8 or 4. If the channel is clean, activate the full lattice. This gives a **channel-adaptive chromatic alphabet** that no fixed-palette system can match.

**Machine-learning classifier.** Instead of nearest-neighbor in CIELAB, train a lightweight classifier (e.g., a small MLP) on the calibration residuals to map received color to the most likely intended symbol. This handles **chroma crosstalk** and **non-Gaussian color noise** that simple distance metrics miss.

---

## 4. Geometric alphabet: from ad hoc shapes to **channel-optimized geometric alphabets**

### The gap

LIBCIMBAR uses **16 shapes** (4 bits) plus 4–8 colors (2–3 bits) per tile, achieving 6–7 bits per tile. But the choice of shapes is **hand-designed**. There is no systematic method for selecting a geometric alphabet that maximizes **mutual information** between transmitted and received shapes under the actual screen-camera channel (blur, perspective, defocus, motion). The result is that some shapes are over-provisioned with robustness while others are fragile.

### Proposed technique: **Channel-Optimized Geometric Alphabet (COGA)**

**Core idea:** Treat the geometric alphabet as a **codebook design problem** and optimize it using a **differentiable rendering pipeline** and **reinforcement learning**.

**Channel model in the loop.** Build a differentiable approximation of the screen-camera channel: display gamma, lens blur (parameterized by \(h_{\text{blur}}\)), perspective warp, sensor noise, and JPEG compression. The OCC literature already models blur as a convolution with a kernel whose spatial extent is \(h_{\text{blur}}\). Make these parameters **stochastic** during optimization so the alphabet is robust across a distribution of channel conditions.

**Alphabet optimization.** Parameterize each shape as a small binary or grayscale template (e.g., 8×8 or 16×16 pixels). Pass all templates through the differentiable channel and compute the **pairwise confusion matrix** at the receiver. The loss is the **negative mutual information** \(I(\text{shape}; \text{received})\) minus a regularization term for visual distinctness. Optimize the templates with gradient descent. This yields shapes that are **maximally distinguishable under blur and noise**, not just visually different to a human.

**Discrete alphabet selection.** After continuous optimization, cluster the optimized templates into a discrete alphabet of size \(q\) (e.g., \(q = 16\)). Ensure that the minimum pairwise distance in the **received feature space** is maximized. The resulting alphabet may include shapes that look similar to humans but are highly separable by the decoder—exactly the opposite of human-centric design.

**Joint optimization with color.** Because PCL and COGA share the same channel, optimize them **jointly**: the combined alphabet is a Cartesian product of geometric and chromatic states, and the mutual information objective accounts for both. This avoids the suboptimality of designing shapes and colors independently.

---

## 5. Fountain coding: from generic RaptorQ to **visual-aware and symbol-importance-aware fountain coding**

### The gap

RaptorQ (RFC 6330) provides near-optimal overhead: **>99% recovery probability with overhead of 0 symbols** in the ideal case, and often just \(K+2\) packets for \(K\) source symbols. AirFerry and QRFerry already use RaptorQ over screen-camera links with ~10% repair rate and demonstrate reliable file transfer. But generic fountain codes treat **all source symbols equally**. In a visual symbol, some bits are inherently more reliable than others: a large geometric shape is more robust than a subtle color difference; a symbol near the center of the frame is less affected by lens distortion than one near the edge. CCRCode recognizes the need for **unequal error protection** but implements it as a separate ring-structured code.

### Proposed technique: **Visual-Aware Fountain Coding (VAFC)**

**Core idea:** Assign each source symbol a **reliability weight** derived from the visual symbol design and the expected channel, and modify the fountain degree distribution so that **low-reliability symbols receive more repair coverage**.

**Reliability weights.** For each source symbol \(j\), define a weight \(w_j \in (0,1]\) that combines:
- **Modulation reliability:** the mutual information of its geometric and chromatic states under COGA/PCL.
- **Spatial reliability:** a function of its position in the frame (edge symbols lower).
- **Semantic importance:** metadata symbols near the root of RFL get higher weight.

**Weighted degree distribution.** Standard LT codes use a degree distribution \(\Omega(d)\) independent of symbol index. VAFC uses a **weighted distribution** \(\Omega_j(d)\) that biases the neighbor selection toward high-weight symbols. This is a fountain-code analogue of **unequal error protection**: high-weight symbols appear in more encoded packets, so they are recovered earlier and more reliably. The decoder can use a **weighted peeling algorithm**: when decoding a packet, prioritize resolving high-weight neighbors.

**Why not just duplicate high-weight symbols?** Duplication is a special case of VAFC with a degenerate degree distribution. VAFC generalizes it and allows **fine-grained allocation** of redundancy without wasting whole packets.

**Compatibility with RFL.** In the Recursive Fountain Lattice, each level has its own VAFC instance. Level 0 uses maximum weighting (all symbols high-reliability), while deeper levels use progressively flatter weighting. This matches the **successive refinement** structure: coarse information is protected heavily; fine information can tolerate more loss because it is optional for basic file recovery.

---

## 6. Hybrid error correction and channel coding: from bit-oriented LDPC to **symbol-metric non-binary hybrid coding**

### The gap

CCRCode’s two-tier approach—**intra-symbol LDPC + inter-symbol rateless with LDPC**—is the state of the art for screen-camera links, achieving **complete recovery in half the time** of competing methods. But its LDPC layer is **binary**. Optical symbol errors are not bit errors: a color confusion or a shape misclassification is a **symbol error** that affects multiple bits simultaneously. Non-binary LDPC (NB-LDPC) over GF(\(q\)) can correct **symbol errors directly** and shows **higher error-correcting performance than binary LDPC**. However, NB-LDPC has not been integrated with a **fountain outer code** for optical screen-camera links, and existing NB-LDPC decoders assume hard or soft bit metrics, not **color-space soft metrics**.

### Proposed technique: **Symbol-Metric Hybrid Coding (SMHC)**

**Core idea:** Build a three-layer hybrid stack where each layer operates on the **native symbol alphabet** and passes **soft symbol metrics** rather than hard bits.

**Layer 1: Soft symbol detector.** The output of PCL/COGA is not a hard decision but a **probability vector over the \(q\)-ary alphabet** for each tile. In CIELAB, compute a softmax over negative \(\Delta E_{00}^*\) to obtain \(P(\text{symbol} = s \mid \text{received color/shape})\).

**Layer 2: Non-binary LDPC over GF(\(q\)).** Use an NB-LDPC code whose field size matches the alphabet size \(q\) (e.g., \(q = 64\) for 6-bit tiles). The decoder is an **FFT-based sum-product algorithm** that accepts the soft symbol probabilities from Layer 1. This corrects **within-frame symbol errors** directly, without converting to bits and losing the correlation between bit errors. Hybrid LDPC constructions can trade off **waterfall performance and error-floor**, which is important for the varying channel conditions of screen-camera links.

**Layer 3: Rateless outer code with symbol erasure.** After NB-LDPC decoding, each tile is either correctly decoded or **erased**. The outer code is a **RaptorQ or LT fountain code over GF(\(q\))**, operating on erasure symbols. Because the inner code already corrects most errors, the outer code sees a **clean erasure channel**, which is exactly the regime where fountain codes are optimal. The outer code provides **cross-frame loss tolerance** without needing to correct residual errors.

**Joint iteration.** Allow **iterative feedback** between Layer 2 and Layer 3: if the outer code’s peeling process reveals that a tile was likely misclassified, it can send an erasure flag back to the inner decoder, which then re-decodes with updated priors. This is a **turbo equalization** approach applied to the optical symbol channel.

**Why this fills the gap:** CCRCode uses LDPC for errors and rateless for erasures, but the two layers are separate and bit-oriented. SMHC makes both layers **symbol-native** and **soft-information-aware**, which is necessary when the alphabet is large (64+ states) and symbol errors dominate.

---

## 7. Alphanumeric and symbolic alphabets: from flat mappings to **hierarchical alphabet partitioning with multi-level coding**

### The gap

The user’s request for “numbers, letters, symbols” is best interpreted at the **coding-alphabet level**: the symbol set should be large and structured, not literally drawn as ASCII characters. Existing high-density codes use flat mappings: LIBCIMBAR maps 16 shapes and 8 colors to a 7-bit integer; JAB Code uses 8 colors with a fixed mapping. A flat mapping treats all symbol transitions equally, but in a perceptual alphabet, **some transitions are much more likely than others** (e.g., adjacent colors in CIELAB are more confusable than distant colors). This is exactly the situation where **coded modulation** and **multi-level coding (MLC)** outperform flat bit-interleaved schemes.

### Proposed technique: **Hierarchical Alphabet Partitioning with Multi-Level Coding (HAP-MLC)**

**Core idea:** Partition the \(q\)-ary alphabet into a **hierarchy of cosets**, protect each partition level with a **different code rate**, and use **set partitioning** to ensure that the most likely confusions occur only within the most heavily protected level.

**Alphabet tree.** Build a tree where the root partitions the \(q\) symbols into \(2^{m_1}\) cosets, each coset partitions into \(2^{m_2}\) sub-cosets, and so on. For a 64-ary alphabet, a natural tree is \(64 = 4 \times 4 \times 4\) (three levels of 2 bits each). Assign the **most significant level** to the coarsest partition (e.g., shape family or color hue family) and the **least significant level** to the finest distinction (e.g., exact shape within a family).

**Set partitioning for confusability.** Design the partition so that symbols within the **same coarsest coset** are the ones most likely to be confused under channel noise. Then the coarsest level carries the most protection: even if fine distinctions are lost, the coarse symbol is recovered. This is the **Ungerboeck set-partitioning principle** applied to optical symbols.

**Multi-level coding.** Encode each level with an independent code. The coarsest level uses a **low-rate NB-LDPC code** (or a repetition/Reed–Solomon code), while finer levels use higher-rate codes. At the decoder, use **multi-stage decoding**: decode the coarsest level first, subtract its contribution, then decode the next level. This is **successive cancellation** in the symbol domain.

**Integration with SMHC.** HAP-MLC replaces the flat NB-LDPC layer in SMHC with a **multi-level NB-LDPC stack**. The soft symbol probabilities from PCL/COGA are marginalized to each level, and the multi-stage decoder operates level by level. This gives **graceful degradation**: under poor channel conditions, only the coarse levels decode and the receiver gets a reduced-rate but valid data stream; under good conditions, all levels decode for full rate.

**Why “alphanumeric” is satisfied:** The alphabet tree can be designed so that coarse levels correspond to **alphanumeric classes** (digits, uppercase, lowercase, punctuation) and fine levels correspond to **specific characters**. This gives a semantic hierarchy that maps naturally to text data, while binary/APK data simply uses the full alphabet uniformly. The hierarchy is **content-adaptive**: text payloads can allocate more protection to coarse levels, while binary payloads use a balanced allocation.

---

## 8. The unified system: **ARCH** (Aperiodic Recursive Chromatic Hybrid)

The individual techniques above are designed to interlock. The complete architecture is:

| Layer | Technique | Function |
|---|---|---|
| **Geometry** | **CMC** (Chiral Monotile Coding) | Aperiodic Spectre tiling with orientation, chirality, and cluster-level symbols |
| **Recursion** | **RFL** (Recursive Fountain Lattice) | Hierarchical fountain layers with successive refinement and structural UEP |
| **Color** | **PCL** (Perceptual Chromatic Lattice) | CIECAM02 alphabet with per-frame calibration and adaptive palette size |
| **Shape** | **COGA** (Channel-Optimized Geometric Alphabet) | Differentiable-rendering-optimized shapes under blur/perspective |
| **Modulation** | **HAP-MLC** | Hierarchical alphabet partitioning with multi-level coding and set partitioning |
| **Inner FEC** | **SMHC** (Layer 2) | Non-binary LDPC over GF(\(q\)) with soft symbol metrics |
| **Outer FEC** | **VAFC** (Visual-Aware Fountain Coding) | Weighted fountain codes with reliability-aware degree distributions |
| **Transport** | **Multi-frame streaming** | 60–120 FPS, parallel tiles, rolling-shutter awareness |

### End-to-end data flow

1. **Ingest** APK or arbitrary binary. Compress with zstd/XZ selection. Split into ~32 MiB segments (AirFerry’s proven segment size).
2. **RFL layering** assigns each segment to a recursion level based on importance and size.
3. **VAFC encoding** produces fountain packets per level with reliability weights.
4. **HAP-MLC + SMHC** encode each packet into \(q\)-ary symbols with multi-level protection and NB-LDPC.
5. **CMC + PCL + COGA** map symbols to Spectre tile configurations, CIELAB colors, and optimized shapes.
6. **Streaming** displays frames at high rate, with parallel tiles per frame.
7. **Receiver** captures frames, applies PCL calibration, detects CMC templates hierarchically, computes soft symbol probabilities, runs SMHC decoding, feeds erasures to VAFC, and reconstructs the file. Verify SHA-256 before delivery.

### Predicted performance envelope

| Metric | Conservative | Aggressive |
|---|---|---|
| Bits per tile | 6–7 (LIBCIMBAR-class) | 8–9 (CMC cluster labels + PCL + COGA) |
| Tiles per frame | ~10⁴ | ~4×10⁴ (higher resolution) |
| Frames per second | 60 | 120 |
| Raw visual throughput | ~36 Mbit/s | ~345 Mbit/s |
| After FEC overhead (~2×) | ~18 Mbit/s (~2.2 MB/s) | ~170 Mbit/s (~21 MB/s) |
| Occlusion resilience | 40% (SSMC baseline) | 40–60% with CMC hierarchical decoding |

Even the conservative estimate delivers **megabytes per second**, making gigabyte transfers a matter of tens of minutes rather than hours.

---

## 9. The single most important gap and the new thing it demands

If one gap must be prioritized above all others, it is this: **no existing optical code co-designs the symbol geometry, the color alphabet, and the error-correction code as a single joint object.** SSMC designs geometry and uses RS/LDPC as an afterthought. NestedDot designs recursion and admits error correction is immature. CCRCode designs coding but uses conventional barcodes as the physical layer. COGA, PCL, HAP-MLC, SMHC, and VAFC are each designed to be **aware of the others**:

- COGA’s shapes are optimized for the **same channel** that PCL’s colors are calibrated on.
- HAP-MLC’s partition tree is built from **COGA/PCL confusion probabilities**.
- SMHC’s NB-LDPC field size is the **Cartesian product of COGA and PCL alphabet sizes**.
- VAFC’s reliability weights are computed from **SMHC’s post-decoding symbol error rates**.
- CMC’s cluster hierarchy provides the **template bank** that RFL uses for successive refinement.

This is the leap: **a fully co-designed optical stack in which the physics of the screen-camera channel, the perception of color and shape, the algebra of aperiodic tiling, and the mathematics of fountain/LDPC coding are treated as one optimization problem rather than a pipeline of independent choices.** That co-design, more than any single technique, is what fills the gap between current research prototypes and a production-grade megabyte-to-gigabyte optical transport system.

"I want to create two different systems. I want a static image that can be used for printing out and other non moving things as well as a 3D rotating mass of shapes, colours, letters, numbers, etc., that are all connected together in a rotating mass that looks enchanting and futuristic and magical that is fast enough to transfer data but slow enough that any device scanning will be able to be able to scan fast enough even with older hardware. Do deep research and provide comprehensive in depth and detailed information"

The two systems you are describing are best understood as **two transport modes that share one codec core**. The static mode trades time for space: it packs data into a printed mosaic that a scanner reads tile by tile. The kinetic mode trades space for time: it spreads data across the faces of a rotating three‑dimensional object and uses **face‑rate matching** so that even an 8 fps camera captures every face exactly once per revolution.

The critical design constraint is that **old cameras are slow, not blind**. Research on screen‑camera links shows that smartphone cameras can range from **8 to 30 fps**, and that reliable communication is possible as long as the receiver frame rate is at least half the transmitter frame rate. For the rotating mass, this becomes a simple kinematic rule: if the object has \(F\) faces and rotates once every \(T\) seconds, the **face presentation rate** is \(F/T\). Set \(F/T \le R_{\text{camera,min}}\). Then an 8 fps camera captures each face at least once per revolution, and the system works without any synchronization handshake.

---

## 1. Shared foundations: one codec core, two transports

Both systems use the same five‑layer codec. Only the physical transport differs.

| Layer | Function | Technology |
|---|---|---|
| **Alphabet** | Dense visual symbol set | **Chromatic–Geometric–Glyph (CGG) alphabet**: 16 geometric shapes × 4–8 colors = 64–128 states; optional alphanumeric glyph mode at larger tile sizes |
| **Inner code** | Within‑tile error correction | Non‑binary LDPC over GF(64) with soft symbol metrics, or Reed–Solomon as a fallback |
| **Outer code** | Loss tolerance | RaptorQ (RFC 6330) or LT fountain codes across tiles/faces |
| **Integrity** | File verification | SHA‑256 before delivery |
| **Android pipeline** | Capture and decode | CameraX + native Rust/C++ decoder pool + fountain decoder |

This shared core means the app can switch between static and kinetic modes without re‑implementing the codec. The CGG alphabet directly satisfies the requirement for shapes, colours, letters, and numbers: geometric shapes and colors carry the dense payload, while alphanumeric glyphs are used in lower‑density “readable” modes or as part of the tile header.

---

## 2. System A — Static Printable Code (SPC): the **Progressive Static Mosaic**

### 2.1 Why a single static image is kilobyte‑scale, not megabyte‑scale

A single high‑density 2D symbol is impressive but bounded. LIBCIMBAR stores **7,500 bytes per 1024×1024 image** after error correction, using 16 shapes and 4–8 colors per tile. JAB Code, an ISO standard, can store **hundreds of kilobytes** in a cascaded symbol and supports up to 64 colors in its specification. Paper‑based storage formats such as PaperBack reach **500 kB on one A4 side at 600 dpi**. HD InfoCode+ claims **225× the data of a QR code** of comparable size, but the company has not published independent side‑by‑side benchmarking.

The practical conclusion is:

- **A single printable tile:** 7.5–24 KB.
- **A single A4 page as a mosaic of independent tiles:** roughly **200–500 KB** at 600–1200 dpi, depending on tile size and color calibration.
- **A multi‑page document:** megabytes, but each page is an independent fountain‑coded unit.

### 2.2 Page architecture: a self‑describing mosaic

Rather than one giant fragile symbol, the page is divided into **independent code tiles** arranged in a grid or hexagonal packing. Each tile is a complete, decodable CGG symbol with its own header and inner LDPC. Around the mosaic are **calibration patches** for color and print‑density correction.

The page has two resolution tiers:

1. **Coarse metadata band** at the top or bottom, printed at larger module size. It contains the file hash, tile count, fountain seed, and a directory of tile indices. An older phone with a lower‑resolution camera can read this band from a distance and confirm what the page contains.
2. **Fine payload tiles** in the main area. Each tile carries a fountain‑coded packet. A high‑resolution scanner or a newer phone reads the tiles individually, and the fountain decoder reconstructs the file from any sufficient subset.

This is the **Progressive Static Mosaic**: old hardware can at least read the metadata and understand the page, while good hardware recovers the full payload.

### 2.3 Tile design and alphabet

A production tile should use:

- **Hexagonal or square tiling.** Hexagonal packing gains roughly 15% density over a square grid at the same module size, but square tiles are easier to print and scan with legacy tools. A square tile with an internal hexagonal sub‑grid is a good compromise.
- **16 geometric shapes × 4–8 colors per cell.** This gives 64–128 states (6–7 bits per cell), matching or exceeding LIBCIMBAR‑class density.
- **Alphanumeric glyphs in the tile header.** The header uses a small set of high‑contrast digits and letters for tile ID, version, and fountain seed. These are decoded with a lightweight OCR classifier, not with the dense shape classifier, so they remain readable even when the payload tiles are slightly blurred.
- **Per‑tile calibration border.** A ring of known color patches surrounds each tile. This allows the scanner to correct for ink variation, paper tint, and lighting without relying on a global page calibration.

### 2.4 Error correction and fountain coding across tiles

Each tile uses **non‑binary LDPC over GF(64)** for within‑tile correction. The page as a whole uses a **RaptorQ or LT fountain code** across tiles. The scanner does not need to read every tile in order. It collects tiles, extracts packets, and feeds them to the fountain decoder. Missing or unreadable tiles are erasures, which fountain codes handle natively.

This is essential for printing because ink bleed, smudges, folds, and glare are unavoidable. A page with 30 tiles might lose 5–10 tiles and still reconstruct the file if the fountain overhead is 1.2–1.5×.

### 2.5 Print and scan requirements

- **Print resolution:** 600 dpi minimum for 8×8‑pixel tiles; 1200 dpi for smaller modules or higher capacity.
- **Paper:** matte, low‑bleed paper. Glossy paper creates specular highlights that destroy color classification.
- **Ink:** CMYK process is acceptable, but a **per‑page calibration chart** must be printed in the margin. The scanner uses it to build a CMYK‑to‑CIELAB transform.
- **Scanning:** a newer phone with macro focus can read fine tiles at 20–30 cm. An older phone with fixed focus may only read the coarse metadata band. This is an intentional graceful degradation, not a failure.

### 2.6 Static mode capacity and time

| Configuration | Capacity per page | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| Single 1024² tile, 4 colors | 7.5 KB | LIBCIMBAR‑class |
| A4 mosaic, 30 tiles, 600 dpi | ~225 KB | 30 × 7.5 KB |
| A4 mosaic, 30 tiles, 8 colors | ~300 KB | Higher color count requires calibration |
| Cascaded JAB‑style page | 200–500 KB | JAB supports hundreds of KB per cascaded symbol |
| Multi‑page document | MB–GB | Each page is an independent fountain unit |

Static mode is not time‑critical. A 1 MB file is 4–5 pages; a 10 MB file is 40–50 pages. This is suitable for archival, air‑gapped, or print‑only distribution, not for instant transfer.

---

## 3. System B — Dynamic Rotating Mass (DRM): the **Face‑Rate‑Matched Kinetic Fountain**

### 3.1 The core insight: match the face rate to the slowest camera

The rotating mass is a three‑dimensional object with \(F\) data‑bearing faces. It rotates once every \(T\) seconds. The **face presentation rate** is:

\[
R_{\text{face}} = \frac{F}{T}
\]

To guarantee that an 8 fps camera captures every face exactly once per revolution:

\[
R_{\text{face}} \le R_{\text{camera,min}} = 8 \text{ fps}
\]

For example:

- \(F = 16\) faces, \(T = 2\) s → \(R_{\text{face}} = 8\) fps.
- \(F = 24\) faces, \(T = 3\) s → \(R_{\text{face}} = 8\) fps.
- \(F = 32\) faces, \(T = 4\) s → \(R_{\text{face}} = 8\) fps.

Under this condition, throughput is **independent of the number of faces**:

\[
\text{Throughput} = R_{\text{face}} \times B_{\text{face}} = R_{\text{camera}} \times B_{\text{face}}
\]

where \(B_{\text{face}}\) is the bytes carried by one face. To increase throughput, you either increase \(B_{\text{face}}\) (denser face code) or use a camera with a higher frame rate. You do not need to spin the object faster and risk motion blur.

This is the **Face‑Rate‑Matched Kinetic Fountain (FRM‑KF)**: a rotating data sculpture that is deliberately slow enough for old hardware, yet carries enough data per face to be useful.

### 3.2 Physical form factor

The mass can be a **polyhedral lantern** or a **cylindrical drum**:

- **16–32 flat faces**, each a rigid acrylic or PCB panel.
- Each face carries a **CGG code tile** of 7.5–24 KB, printed or embedded as a high‑contrast pattern.
- Faces are connected by **structural struts** that also carry synchronization markers and calibration patches. The struts make the object look like a connected 3D lattice rather than a loose collection of panels.
- A **central axle and motor** rotate the mass at 0.25–0.5 Hz. The motor can be controlled by the Android app over Bluetooth, or the mass can be a passive object rotated by hand at a steady speed.
- **Edge‑lit acrylic or phosphorescent materials** make the object visually enchanting. The data patterns remain high‑contrast; the decorative lighting is placed behind or around the code areas so it does not reduce symbol contrast.

For a screen‑based version, the same geometry can be rendered on a **cylindrical or spherical volumetric display** or on a flat screen using a rotating 3D illusion. The face rate is then set in software, which makes it trivial to match any camera fps.

### 3.3 Face code design

Each face is a **complete independent fountain packet**:

- **Header:** face ID, fountain seed, file hash prefix, and a small number of calibration patches.
- **Payload:** a CGG tile with 16 shapes × 4–8 colors, protected by inner LDPC.
- **Footer:** CRC and a rotation‑direction marker so the decoder knows which face is which.

The face code can be larger than a single LIBCIMBAR tile because the face is physically larger. A 10 cm × 10 cm face at 600 dpi can hold multiple 1024² tiles. For old‑hardware compatibility, however, it is better to keep the module size large enough that an 8 fps camera with a modest lens can resolve it. A practical starting point is **one 1024² tile per face**, giving 7.5 KB per face.

### 3.4 Fountain coding across faces

The rotating mass is a **fountain‑coded sequence of faces**. The encoder takes the file, applies RaptorQ or LT coding, and assigns one encoded packet to each face. The decoder collects faces as they appear, feeds the packets to the fountain decoder, and reconstructs the file from any sufficient set.

Because the face rate is matched to the camera, the receiver does not need to request retransmissions and does not need to know the rotation phase. It simply detects faces as they appear, decodes them, and accumulates packets. If a face is blurred, occluded, or missed, it is an erasure. The fountain code handles it.

This is the same principle used in unsynchronized screen‑camera links: frame loss is absorbed by rateless coding rather than by synchronization. The rotating mass extends this to a physical object.

### 3.5 Old‑hardware compatibility

The FRM‑KF design is explicitly backward‑compatible:

| Camera fps | Face rate | Faces per revolution | Bytes per face | Raw throughput |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 8 fps | 8 fps | 16 (T=2 s) | 7.5 KB | 60 KB/s |
| 15 fps | 15 fps | 30 (T=2 s) | 7.5 KB | 112.5 KB/s |
| 30 fps | 30 fps | 30 (T=1 s) | 7.5 KB | 225 KB/s |

With a 1.2–1.5× fountain overhead, effective throughput is roughly **40–50 KB/s on an 8 fps camera**, **75–95 KB/s on a 15 fps camera**, and **150–190 KB/s on a 30 fps camera**. That is fast enough for files and slow enough for old hardware.

If the face capacity is increased to 24 KB (JAB‑class), these numbers triple: **120 KB/s raw at 8 fps**, **360 KB/s raw at 15 fps**, and **720 KB/s raw at 30 fps**. The limit is then the camera’s ability to resolve the denser face code, not the rotation speed.

### 3.6 Motion blur and exposure

At 0.5 Hz rotation, a face moves slowly. During a 1/30 s exposure, the angular displacement is small enough that motion blur is negligible for a face at the outer radius. If the mass rotates faster for higher‑fps cameras, the app can shorten exposure or use a strobe backlight synchronized to face presentation. The old‑hardware mode deliberately uses slow rotation and longer exposure to maximize contrast.

### 3.7 Decoding pipeline

1. **Detect the mass** in the camera frame using a lightweight object detector or contour finder.
2. **Identify the face** using the header and rotation‑direction marker.
3. **De‑warp** the face using the four corner markers or the strut sync patterns.
4. **Calibrate color** using the per‑face patches.
5. **Decode the CGG tile** with the soft‑symbol LDPC decoder.
6. **Feed the packet** to the fountain decoder.
7. **When sufficient packets are collected**, reconstruct, verify SHA‑256, and deliver the file.

The Android app uses CameraX with `STRATEGY_KEEP_ONLY_LATEST` to avoid frame queuing, and a native decoder pool to process faces in parallel. The rotation motor is optional; the app can also guide the user to rotate the mass by hand at the correct speed using a visual metronome.

---

## 4. Shared visual alphabet: the CGG code

Both systems use the same **Chromatic–Geometric–Glyph** alphabet.

| Mode | Symbol set | Bits per cell | Use case |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Dense** | 16 shapes × 4 colors | 6 bits | Static payload tiles, rotating faces |
| **Dense+** | 16 shapes × 8 colors | 7 bits | High‑quality print or bright display |
| **Readable** | 10 digits + 26 letters + 16 shapes | ~5.7 bits | Headers, metadata bands, human‑readable labels |

The dense modes are for machine‑only payload. The readable mode uses actual alphanumeric glyphs at larger sizes for headers and for applications where a human may need to inspect the code. The decoder switches between modes based on the tile header.

This directly satisfies the requirement for a mixture of shapes, symbols, numbers, and letters without sacrificing density in the payload regions.

---

## 5. Capacity and transfer time

### 5.1 Static mode

| File size | Pages needed (at 250 KB/page) | Scan time (rough) |
|---|---|---|
| 1 MB | 4–5 pages | 2–5 minutes |
| 10 MB | 40–50 pages | 20–50 minutes |
| 100 MB | 400–500 pages | Several hours |

Static mode is for archival and print distribution, not for rapid transfer.

### 5.2 Kinetic mode

| Camera fps | Bytes per face | Raw throughput | Effective throughput (1.3× overhead) | Time for 10 MB |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 8 fps | 7.5 KB | 60 KB/s | ~46 KB/s | ~3.6 minutes |
| 15 fps | 7.5 KB | 112.5 KB/s | ~86 KB/s | ~1.9 minutes |
| 30 fps | 7.5 KB | 225 KB/s | ~173 KB/s | ~1 minute |
| 8 fps | 24 KB | 192 KB/s | ~148 KB/s | ~1.1 minutes |
| 15 fps | 24 KB | 360 KB/s | ~277 KB/s | ~36 seconds |

The kinetic mode is the practical path to **megabyte‑scale transfer with old hardware**. Gigabyte transfer is a matter of minutes to tens of minutes, depending on camera fps and face density.

---

## 6. Android implementation blueprint

### 6.1 Kotlin app structure

- **Mode selector:** Static Scan / Kinetic Scan.
- **CameraX `ImageAnalysis`** with `STRATEGY_KEEP_ONLY_LATEST`.
- **Native decoder pool** in Rust exposed via JNI. Rust gives C‑like performance and memory safety for the multithreaded decoder.
- **Fountain decoder** in Kotlin or Rust, depending on performance needs.
- **File I/O** with `ContentResolver` for APK and arbitrary binary files.
- **SHA‑256 verification** before exposing the reconstructed file.
- **Motor control** via Bluetooth LE for the kinetic mode (optional).

### 6.2 Static scanning pipeline

1. Capture page image.
2. Detect the coarse metadata band and read it with the readable CGG mode.
3. Detect individual tiles using the calibration borders.
4. Decode each tile with the dense CGG decoder.
5. Feed packets to the fountain decoder.
6. Reconstruct and verify.

### 6.3 Kinetic scanning pipeline

1. Capture frames continuously.
2. Detect the rotating mass and segment the visible face.
3. Read the face header and rotation marker.
4. Decode the face tile.
5. Feed the packet to the fountain decoder.
6. Display progress as faces are accumulated.
7. Reconstruct when enough packets are received.

### 6.4 Adaptive behavior

- If the camera fps is low, the app instructs the motor to rotate at the matched speed.
- If the camera fps is high, the app can increase rotation speed or use more faces.
- If decoding fails repeatedly, the app can switch from dense CGG to readable CGG for that face, trading capacity for robustness.

---

## 7. Novel techniques introduced

1. **Progressive Static Mosaic (PSM).** A printed page with a coarse metadata band readable by old hardware and fine payload tiles readable by high‑resolution scanners, unified by cross‑tile fountain coding.
2. **Face‑Rate‑Matched Kinetic Fountain (FRM‑KF).** A rotating 3D mass whose face presentation rate is deliberately matched to the slowest supported camera, making throughput equal to \(R_{\text{camera}} \times B_{\text{face}}\) and guaranteeing capture of every face without synchronization.
3. **Chromatic–Geometric–Glyph (CGG) alphabet.** A single alphabet family that spans dense machine‑only shapes and colors, human‑readable alphanumeric glyphs, and intermediate modes.
4. **Dual‑resolution synchronization.** Coarse sync markers for old cameras and fine per‑face markers for high‑resolution decoding, allowing the same object to serve both hardware tiers.

---

## 8. Risks and mitigations

| Risk | Mitigation |
|---|---|
| **Motion blur on rotating mass** | Slow rotation (0.25–0.5 Hz), short exposure, optional strobe backlight |
| **Color shift under different lighting** | Per‑face and per‑page calibration patches; CIELAB classification |
| **Print bleed and ink variation** | Inner LDPC, minimum module size, matte paper, 600–1200 dpi |
| **Old camera fixed focus** | Large faces, coarse metadata band, user guidance to maintain distance |
| **Face misidentification** | Header with face ID and rotation marker; fountain coding tolerates erasures |
| **Thermal throttling on old phones** | Native decoder efficiency, frame dropping, adaptive fps |

---

## 9. Conclusion

The optimal two‑system design is a **shared CGG codec** with two transports:

- **Static:** a **Progressive Static Mosaic** that packs 200–500 KB per A4 page, with a coarse metadata band for old hardware and fine fountain‑coded tiles for high‑resolution scanning. It is for printing, archival, and air‑gapped distribution.
- **Kinetic:** a **Face‑Rate‑Matched Kinetic Fountain**, a rotating 3D mass of connected shape‑, color‑, and glyph‑bearing faces. By setting the face rate to 8 fps, even the oldest smartphone camera captures every face once per revolution. Each face carries a fountain packet, so missed faces are erasures rather than failures. Throughput scales directly with camera fps and face capacity: **~46 KB/s effective on an 8 fps camera with 7.5 KB faces, ~150 KB/s with 24 KB faces**, and proportionally higher on better cameras.

This is the leap: not a bigger static QR code, and not a faster‑than‑camera animation, but a **kinetic fountain whose speed is dictated by the weakest camera in the room**, wrapped in a visual object that is genuinely enchanting and futuristic.

"Design and develop purpose built AI/ML models for all aspects of the pipelines tooling systems and rest of the project. Do deep research and provide in depth and detail suggestions"The AI/ML layer should not be a collection of independent classifiers bolted onto a codec. It must be a **co-designed cognitive control plane** that perceives the optical channel, predicts its behaviour, allocates redundancy where it will be most effective, and decodes under conditions where classical algorithms collapse. Every model below is specified by its task, architecture, training signal, deployment target, and—critically—its **interface to the rest of the stack**.

---

## 1. The model portfolio: a single view

| # | Model | Pipeline | Task | Architecture family | Deployment |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **M1** | **Decodability Oracle** | Shared / capture gate | Predict whether a frame or tile is worth decoding | Lightweight CNN or gradient-boosted tree | CPU / NNAPI |
| **M2** | **Tile Localiser** | Static + Kinetic | Find and rectify code regions under perspective and occlusion | YOLO-family detector + keypoint head | GPU / NNAPI |
| **M3** | **Super-Resolution Restorer** | Static + Kinetic | Upscale and deblur low-resolution captures | GAN / diffusion-lite / multi-step SR | GPU |
| **M4** | **Chromatic Calibrator** | Static + Kinetic | Learn per-frame colour transform from pilots to CIELAB | Unsupervised feed-forward net (E2E-FNN) or tiny MLP | CPU |
| **M5** | **Shape–Colour Classifier** | Both | Map rectified tiles to soft symbol probabilities | Zero-overhead ANN or compact ViT | Zero-overhead inference |
| **M6** | **Neural Inner Decoder** | Both | Correct within-frame symbol errors (NB-LDPC) | Boosted neural min-sum or message-passing transformer | CPU / DSP |
| **M7** | **Fountain Degree Optimiser** | Both | Choose LT/RaptorQ degree distributions for the current channel | Deep reinforcement learning (DQN/PPO) | On-device or cloud-trained, on-device inference |
| **M8** | **Face Tracker & Rotation Estimator** | Kinetic | Track the rotating mass, estimate face phase, predict blur | Recurrent CNN or temporal transformer | GPU / NNAPI |
| **M9** | **Motion Deblurrer** | Kinetic | Remove rotation and rolling-shutter blur from faces | Deformable CNN or video transformer | GPU |
| **M10** | **Motor Synchroniser** | Kinetic | Control rotation speed to match camera fps and minimise blur | RL (SAC/DDPG) or model-predictive control with learned dynamics | MCU / BLE bridge |
| **M11** | **Channel State Transformer** | Shared | Estimate blur, colour shift, SNR, and erasure rate from recent frames | Small transformer or GRU | CPU |
| **M12** | **Print Defect Inspector** | Static production | Classify printed mosaics before distribution | Lightweight ViT with hybrid attention | Cloud / edge GPU |
| **M13** | **Synthetic Channel Generator** | Training infrastructure | Produce realistic training data across print, capture, and rotation conditions | GAN / differentiable renderer | Training only |

The rest of this document specifies each model in depth and then describes how they are trained and deployed together.

---

## 2. Perception models (M1–M3): seeing the code before decoding it

### M1. Decodability Oracle (pre-decoding filter)

**Gap.** Classical decoders waste compute on frames that are hopeless: severe blur, specular glare, extreme perspective, or partial occlusion. A recent patent describes a **decodability machine learning model (MLM)** trained to predict whether a captured image will be decodable before invoking the full decoder, so that only promising frames reach the expensive path. This is exactly the right architecture for a system that must run on older phones with limited thermal headroom.

**Architecture.** A compact CNN (e.g., MobileNetV3-Small backbone with a global pooling head) that takes a downscaled frame (e.g., 224×224) and outputs a scalar decodability score plus a **blur / contrast / occlusion** regression head. The auxiliary heads act as auxiliary supervision and make the model useful even when the decodability label is noisy. For an even lighter option, a gradient-boosted tree on handcrafted features (Laplacian variance, edge density, colour variance in CIELAB) can run in under a millisecond, but the CNN is more robust to the complex degradations of screen-camera links.

**Training signal.** Generate positive examples by running the full classical decoder and labelling frames that succeed. Generate negatives by aggressively degrading successful frames until decoding fails. Use **hard-negative mining**: frames that the oracle incorrectly marks as decodable are added back to training. The patent’s framing—predicting “decodability by the barcode-decoding module”—means the oracle is trained on the **specific decoder’s** failure modes, not on a generic “image quality” score.

**Deployment.** Run on the CPU or NNAPI at 30–60 fps on downscaled frames. If the score is below a threshold, skip the frame entirely. This alone can cut decoder power draw by 50–70% in poor lighting.

### M2. Tile Localiser

**Gap.** High-density aperiodic and hexagonal symbols lack the finder patterns that make QR localisation trivial. Classical contour detection fails under partial occlusion and on curved printed surfaces. YOLO-family detectors are already used in production barcode scanning (e.g., a Flutter barcode package powered by YOLO object detection and ZXing decoding), and industrial systems use YOLOv8 for barcode localisation.

**Architecture.** A YOLO-style single-stage detector with a **keypoint regression head** that predicts the four corners (or six vertices for hexagonal tiles) of each tile. Use a lightweight backbone (YOLOv8n or a custom MobileNet-based neck) and train with a combined loss: objectness + bounding box + keypoint heatmap. For aperiodic tilings, the keypoint head should predict **tile centroid and orientation**, not axis-aligned boxes, because tiles are not rectangular.

**Training data.** Synthetic mosaics rendered on curved, tilted, and partially occluded surfaces, with realistic print bleed and lighting. Add **negative tiles** that look code-like but are not (decorative patterns, text blocks) to reduce false positives. For the kinetic mode, include motion-blurred and rolling-shutter-distorted tiles.

**Deployment.** GPU or NNAPI. The detector does not need to run on every frame at full resolution: run it on a downscaled frame, then crop and rectify at full resolution only around detected tiles.

### M3. Super-Resolution Restorer

**Gap.** Old phone cameras and distant scans produce tiles with too few pixels per module. Mosaic-SR addresses this directly: an adaptive multi-step super-resolution method for low-resolution 2D barcodes, motivated by distant or small barcodes yielding low-pixel-density images. A blurred barcode processing method fused with super-resolution restoration claims a decoding success rate over 98%. ESRTMDet combines super-resolution with real-time object detection for industrial 2D barcodes. QR-CNN/QRGAN architectures have also been proposed for QR enhancement.

**Architecture.** Do **not** use a single generic SR model. Use a **two-stage restorer**:

1. **Degradation-aware SR** (Mosaic-SR style): a small CNN that takes the low-resolution tile and the estimated blur kernel from M11 and produces a 2× or 4× upscaled tile. Train with synthetic pairs generated by the channel generator (M13).
2. **Symbol-aware refinement**: a GAN or diffusion-lite refiner that sharpens module edges and restores colour saturation. The discriminator should be conditioned on the symbol alphabet (shape + colour) so that the refiner does not hallucinate plausible but incorrect symbols. This is critical: a GAN that invents edges can **decrease** decode rate. Use a **decode-in-the-loop loss**: pass the restored tile through the differentiable classifier (M5) and penalise cases where the restored tile’s predicted symbol differs from the ground truth.

**Training.** Synthetic low-resolution / high-resolution pairs from the differentiable channel model, plus real paired data captured by placing a high-resolution camera and a low-resolution camera side by side. Fine-tune on the target device’s actual camera characteristics.

**Deployment.** GPU. Run only when M1 signals that a frame is borderline decodable and M2 has found a tile. This makes SR an **on-demand** stage, not a per-frame cost.

---

## 3. Colour and symbol models (M4–M5): turning pixels into probabilities

### M4. Chromatic Calibrator

**Gap.** Screen-camera colour is unstable: display gamut, camera white balance, ambient light, and JPEG chroma subsampling all shift the received colour point. Existing work uses an **unsupervised end-to-end feed-forward neural network (E2E-FNN)** for colour discrepancy adjustment in display-camera communication. Related work uses unsupervised radial basis function networks for adaptive chromaticity drift mitigation in uniform colour space, and data-aided colour shift keying with ANN-based classification for 4/8/16-colour signals.

**Architecture.** A small MLP (3–5 layers, 64–128 units) that takes the **received CIELAB coordinates of the calibration pilots** and predicts a transform to the intended alphabet coordinates. The transform can be affine (12 parameters) or a low-order polynomial (30–60 parameters). Train **unsupervised**: minimise the distance between transformed pilot colours and the nearest alphabet point, plus a smoothness regulariser so that the transform does not overfit noise. At inference, apply the transform to every tile in the frame.

**Why unsupervised.** You cannot ship labelled data for every lighting condition. The pilots provide self-supervision: the system knows the intended pilot colours, so the transform that maps received to intended is learnable without any external labels.

**Deployment.** CPU, sub-millisecond. Run once per frame on the pilot patches, not per tile.

### M5. Shape–Colour Classifier (soft symbol detector)

**Gap.** Classical nearest-neighbour classification in CIELAB fails when shape and colour interact (e.g., a blurry triangle can look like a blurred square with a colour shift). 4SQR-CodeScanner demonstrates that a **zero-overhead ANN** can decode four-state geometric QR codes at **100% accuracy on synthetic and real data**, with **~6 MB memory** and **~65 ms decoding time**, by loading weights directly and performing optimised matrix operations without a deep learning runtime. This is the right deployment philosophy for a production Android decoder.

**Architecture.** A compact feed-forward network or tiny ViT that takes a rectified tile (e.g., 16×16 or 32×32 RGB or CIELAB) and outputs a **probability vector over the \(q\)-ary alphabet**. Use **label smoothing** and **temperature scaling** so that the output is a well-calibrated soft metric for the neural inner decoder (M6). For the readable glyph mode (alphanumeric headers), use a separate small OCR head trained on digits and letters at large module sizes.

**Training.** Synthetic tiles rendered through the differentiable channel (M13), with **hard examples** generated by taking real captures that the classical decoder misclassified. Use **mixup** in CIELAB space and **colour jitter** that respects the calibration transform, so the classifier becomes robust to residual colour error.

**Deployment.** Zero-overhead inference: export weights as a flat binary, implement the forward pass in Rust/C++ with hand-tuned matrix multiplication. Target <10 ms per tile on a mid-range 2020 phone. This is the single most latency-critical model in the pipeline.

---

## 4. Error-correction models (M6–M7): learning to correct and to allocate

### M6. Neural Inner Decoder (NB-LDPC)

**Gap.** Classical belief propagation is excellent for long codes but suffers from **error floors** at short-to-medium block lengths and under the correlated symbol errors of optical channels. Recent work shows two viable paths:

- **Boosted Neural Min-Sum (NMS)**: a decoder that operates identically to conventional NMS but is trained with uncorrected-vector boosting, block-wise scheduling, dynamic weight sharing, transfer learning, and data augmentation. It achieves **state-of-the-art error-floor reduction** and meets 6G xURLLC requirements for 5G LDPC codes.
- **Message-passing transformers**: a transformer decoder that integrates belief-propagation principles with differential attention and a differentiable syndrome loss, outperforming traditional BP for short-to-medium LDPC codes.

**Architecture for this system.** Use a **two-tier neural decoder**:

1. **Boosted NMS** for the bulk inner code. It is lightweight, has no runtime DL dependency after training, and can be implemented as a modified min-sum decoder with learned offset/slope parameters. Train on **symbol-level** errors generated by M5’s confusion matrix, not on generic AWGN.
2. **Message-passing transformer** only for short, critical blocks (e.g., the RFL root layer carrying file metadata). This layer can afford higher latency because it is decoded once, not per tile.

**Training signal.** The decoder should be trained on **soft symbol probabilities from M5**, not hard bits. Use a **syndrome loss** (differentiable) plus a cross-entropy loss on the decoded message. Curriculum learning: start with low noise, gradually increase to the target error rate, and include **burst error** patterns that mimic smudges, glare, and rolling-shutter artifacts.

**Deployment.** CPU or DSP. The boosted NMS decoder is a few hundred learned parameters, so it can run in real time per tile. The transformer decoder for root blocks can run on NNAPI or GPU.

### M7. Fountain Degree Optimiser

**Gap.** LT/RaptorQ degree distributions are designed for generic erasure channels. In an optical link, the erasure pattern is **structured**: edge tiles fail more often than centre tiles; faces at certain rotation phases blur more; some colours are more confusable than others. Deep reinforcement learning has been used to **optimise LT degree distributions**, and a GNN-based decoder has been proposed for analog fountain codes. The gap is a **channel-aware degree optimiser** that uses the current erasure statistics from M11 to select or adapt the distribution.
